Designs and iterates production-grade frontend interfaces. Real working code, committed design choices, exceptional craft.

## Setup (non-optional)

Before any design work or file edits, pass these gates. Skipping them produces generic output that ignores the project.

| Gate | Required check | If fail |
|---|---|---|
| Context | The PRODUCT.md / DESIGN.md loader result is known from `node .agents/skills/impeccable/scripts/load-context.mjs`. | Run the loader before continuing. |
| Product | PRODUCT.md exists and is not empty or placeholder (`[TODO]` markers, <200 chars). | Run `$impeccable teach`, refresh context, then resume. Never synthesize PRODUCT.md from the user's original prompt alone. |
| Command | The matching command reference is loaded when a sub-command is used. | Load the reference before continuing. |
| Craft | `$impeccable craft` has a user-confirmed shape brief for this task. `teach` / PRODUCT.md never counts as shape. | Run `$impeccable shape` and wait for explicit brief confirmation. |
| Image | Required visual probes / mocks are generated or skipped with a reason. | Resolve the image-generation gate in `shape.md` or `craft.md` before code. |
| Mutation | All active gates above pass. | Do not edit project files yet. |

Codex-style agents must state this before editing files:

```text
IMPECCABLE_PREFLIGHT: context=pass product=pass command_reference=pass shape=pass|not_required image_gate=pass|skipped:<reason> mutation=open
```

For `$impeccable craft`, `shape=pass` is only valid after a separate user response approving the shape design brief, or when the user provided an already-confirmed brief in the request. Do not mark `shape=pass` after writing PRODUCT.md, summarizing assumptions, or drafting an unconfirmed brief yourself.

Other harnesses should follow the same checklist when they can expose this state.

### 1. Context gathering

Two files, case-insensitive. The loader looks at the project root by default and falls back to `.agents/context/` and `docs/` if the root is clean. Override with `IMPECCABLE_CONTEXT_DIR=path/to/dir` (absolute or relative to cwd).

- **PRODUCT.md** — required. Users, brand, tone, anti-references, strategic principles.
- **DESIGN.md** — optional, strongly recommended. Colors, typography, elevation, components.

Load both in one call:

```bash
node .agents/skills/impeccable/scripts/load-context.mjs
```

Consume the full JSON output. Never pipe through `head`, `tail`, `grep`, or `jq`. The output's `contextDir` field tells you where the files were resolved from.

If the output is already in this session's conversation history, don't re-run. Exceptions requiring a fresh load: you just ran `$impeccable teach` or `$impeccable document` (they rewrite the files), or the user manually edited one.

`$impeccable live` already warms context via `live.mjs` — if you've run `live.mjs`, don't also run `load-context.mjs` this session.

If PRODUCT.md is missing, empty, or placeholder (`[TODO]` markers, <200 chars): run `$impeccable teach`, then resume the user's original task with the fresh context. If the original task was `$impeccable craft`, resume into `$impeccable shape` before any implementation work.

If DESIGN.md is missing: nudge once per session (*"Run `$impeccable document` for more on-brand output"*), then proceed.

### 2. Register

Every design task is **brand** (marketing, landing, campaign, long-form content, portfolio — design IS the product) or **product** (app UI, admin, dashboard, tool — design SERVES the product).

Identify before designing. Priority: (1) cue in the task itself ("landing page" vs "dashboard"); (2) the surface in focus (the page, file, or route being worked on); (3) `register` field in PRODUCT.md. First match wins.

If PRODUCT.md lacks the `register` field (legacy), infer it once from its "Users" and "Product Purpose" sections, then cache the inferred value for the session. Suggest the user run `$impeccable teach` to add the field explicitly.

Load the matching reference: [reference/brand.md](reference/brand.md) or [reference/product.md](reference/product.md). The shared design laws below apply to both.

## Shared design laws

Apply to every design, both registers. Match implementation complexity to the aesthetic vision — maximalism needs elaborate code, minimalism needs precision. Interpret creatively. Vary across projects; never converge on the same choices. GPT is capable of extraordinary work — don't hold back.

### Color

- Use OKLCH. Reduce chroma as lightness approaches 0 or 100 — high chroma at extremes looks garish.
- Never use `#000` or `#fff`. Tint every neutral toward the brand hue (chroma 0.005–0.01 is enough).
- Pick a **color strategy** before picking colors. Four steps on the commitment axis:
  - **Restrained** — tinted neutrals + one accent ≤10%. Product default; brand minimalism.
  - **Committed** — one saturated color carries 30–60% of the surface. Brand default for identity-driven pages.
  - **Full palette** — 3–4 named roles, each used deliberately. Brand campaigns; product data viz.
  - **Drenched** — the surface IS the color. Brand heroes, campaign pages.
- The "one accent ≤10%" rule is Restrained only. Committed / Full palette / Drenched exceed it on purpose. Don't collapse every design to Restrained by reflex.

### Theme

Dark vs. light is never a default. Not dark "because tools look cool dark." Not light "to be safe."

Before choosing, write one sentence of physical scene: who uses this, where, under what ambient light, in what mood. If the sentence doesn't force the answer, it's not concrete enough — add detail until it does.

"Observability dashboard" does not force an answer. "SRE glancing at incident severity on a 27-inch monitor at 2am in a dim room" does. Run the sentence, not the category.

### Typography

- Cap body line length at 65–75ch.
- Hierarchy through scale + weight contrast (≥1.25 ratio between steps). Avoid flat scales.

### Layout

- Vary spacing for rhythm. Same padding everywhere is monotony.
- Cards are the lazy answer. Use them only when they're truly the best affordance. Nested cards are always wrong.
- Don't wrap everything in a container. Most things don't need one.

### Motion

- Don't animate CSS layout properties.
- Ease out with exponential curves (ease-out-quart / quint / expo). No bounce, no elastic.

### Absolute bans

Match-and-refuse. If you're about to write any of these, rewrite the element with different structure.

- **Side-stripe borders.** `border-left` or `border-right` greater than 1px as a colored accent on cards, list items, callouts, or alerts. Never intentional. Rewrite with full borders, background tints, leading numbers/icons, or nothing.
- **Gradient text.** `background-clip: text` combined with a gradient background. Decorative, never meaningful. Use a single solid color. Emphasis via weight or size.
- **Glassmorphism as default.** Blurs and glass cards used decoratively. Rare and purposeful, or nothing.
- **The hero-metric template.** Big number, small label, supporting stats, gradient accent. SaaS cliché.
- **Identical card grids.** Same-sized cards with icon + heading + text, repeated endlessly.
- **Modal as first thought.** Modals are usually laziness. Exhaust inline / progressive alternatives first.

### Copy

- Every word earns its place. No restated headings, no intros that repeat the title.
- **No em dashes.** Use commas, colons, semicolons, periods, or parentheses. Also not `--`.

### The AI slop test

If someone could look at this interface and say "AI made that" without doubt, it's failed. Cross-register failures are the absolute bans above. Register-specific failures live in each reference.

**Category-reflex check.** Run at two altitudes — the second one catches what the first one misses.

- **First-order:** if someone could guess the theme + palette from the category alone — "observability → dark blue", "healthcare → white + teal", "finance → navy + gold", "crypto → neon on black" — it's the first training-data reflex. Rework the scene sentence and color strategy until the answer isn't obvious from the domain.
- **Second-order:** if someone could guess the aesthetic family from category-plus-anti-references — "AI workflow tool that's not SaaS-cream → editorial-typographic", "fintech that's not navy-and-gold → terminal-native dark mode" — it's the trap one tier deeper. The first reflex was avoided; the second wasn't. Rework until both answers are not obvious. The brand register's [reflex-reject aesthetic lanes](reference/brand.md) list catches the currently-saturated families.

Plus two management commands — `pin <command>` and `unpin <command>`, detailed below.

### Routing rules

1. **No argument** — render the table above as the user-facing command menu, grouped by category. Ask what they'd like to do.
2. **First word matches a command** — load its reference file and follow its instructions. Everything after the command name is the target.
3. **First word doesn't match** — general design invocation. Apply the setup steps, shared design laws, and the loaded register reference, using the full argument as context.

Setup (context gathering, register) is already loaded by then; sub-commands don't re-invoke `$impeccable`.

If the first word is `craft`, setup still runs first, but [reference/craft.md](reference/craft.md) owns the rest of the flow. If setup invokes `teach` as a blocker, finish teach, refresh context, then resume the original command and target.

## Pin / Unpin

**Pin** creates a standalone shortcut so `$<command>` invokes `$impeccable <command>` directly. **Unpin** removes it. The script writes to every harness directory present in the project.

```bash
node .agents/skills/impeccable/scripts/pin.mjs <pin|unpin> <command>
```

Valid `<command>` is any command from the table above. Report the script's result concisely — confirm the new shortcut on success, relay stderr verbatim on error.

## Operating Context

The platform sits inside a coaching practice that runs partly outside it. Future work must not assume the app is the whole system.

- Coaching sessions happen on video and are transcribed. Session records, notes, and recaps originate outside the app.
- Booking and payment run through their own flow (packages, booking passes, a public booking microsite, single sessions).
- Students also practice on third-party platforms. Work done elsewhere is real work, and the app is not the only record of a student's effort.
- Joshua authors material ahead of time and assigns it, rather than the system generating it on demand.
- Students use both desktop and mobile, in focused study blocks and in short check-ins.

## Capabilities and Constraints

Confirmed surfaces: authentication and onboarding, the foyer, the classroom, guided drills and the question bank, full-passage Reading Comprehension, the Activity Centre, the Testing Centre and practice test runs, the wrong answer journal, saved sets, analytics and history, scheduling and booking, the inbox, the bootcamps, and an admin and teacher workspace.

Technical constraints:

- React, TypeScript, Vite, Tailwind, shadcn/ui and Radix primitives, React Router, TanStack Query, Supabase.
- One motion library (`framer-motion`). One primary icon library (`lucide-react`). New work composes existing primitives before introducing a new dependency.
- Deployment is not a side effect of pushing to `main`. Production at lsatprep.study requires an explicit publish step.
- Database migrations in `supabase/migrations/` are an archive, not an applied ledger. Production schema changes go through their own path, and UI must not assume a table exists because a migration file does.

Terminology is not defined here. It is defined per bounded context and mapped by `CONTEXT-MAP.md`. A word can carry different weight in different contexts, and collapsing them has already caused real bugs (a **flag** is not a **wrong answer**; **Set** and **Position** were separated for this reason).

## Brand Commitments

- **Name:** LSAT U, operating under Aspiring Attorneys. Production domain lsatprep.study.
- **No em-dashes in anything a student or prospect reads.** Joshua does not type them. A stray em-dash reads as machine-written and costs trust. This binds computed copy, utility copy, and authored copy alike.
- **Voice: undecided, deliberately.** The previous record carried a "coach voice, not industry voice" principle and two named voice registers (R1 procedural, R2 metaphor-led). Both were removed on 2026-09-05 by Joshua's decision, on the grounds that they were not stated clearly enough to be usable. They are being redone. Until a replacement is written, no work should cite the old voice rules, and no work should invent a voice rule to fill the gap. Route student-facing copy to Joshua.

## Evidence on Hand

Real:

- A live production deployment at lsatprep.study with real enrolled students.
- Authored LSAT curriculum: bootcamp modules, guided drill banks, RC passages with per-question explanations, and practice test content.
- Real student work in the database: attempts, sessions, flags, wrong answers, assignments.
- A real named instructor with a real coaching practice behind the product.

Absent, and never to be fabricated:

- No testimonials, case studies, press coverage, score-improvement statistics, or named customer references exist in this repo. Do not write them into any surface.
- No pricing, licensing, or availability claims are established here.
- No third-party or copyright framing anywhere in student-facing copy.

## Product Principles

1. **Truthfulness over performance.** Every number, badge, streak, status, and progress claim traces to real stored data. An honest empty state beats invented content. If nothing has been assigned, the product says so and stops. This is the one principle carried forward unchanged from the previous record, and it is the hardest of the five.

2. **Compute the next step; do not present a grid.** A student arriving at a surface should be told what to do, not shown a menu and left to choose. A card grid is what a product offers when it does not know the student.

3. **Attempt before explanation.** The student commits to an answer before the product explains anything. Passive reading does not build the skill. Reveal copy addresses what the student actually chose, not a generic key.

4. **A measurement is never a gate.** Drills and checks measure after teaching. A miss is framed as the next thing to work on, not a grade or a failure. Nothing in the product rewards attendance rather than work.

5. **The coach is not simulated.** Where the product speaks for Joshua, it is because Joshua said it. Where he has not, the product does not fill the silence with something that sounds like him. This is the operational form of the positioning claim.

## Accessibility & Inclusion

**WCAG 2.1 AA is a binding floor across every surface.** It is a defect gate, not an aspiration. A finding below the AA bar is a bug and is fixed, not argued.

- Text contrast is at least 4.5:1 against its actual rendered background, and at least 3:1 for large text and for the visual boundaries of interactive controls. Students read dense argument text on this platform for hours at a time.
- Every interactive element has a visible focus indicator.
- `prefers-reduced-motion` is respected. Motion is never load-bearing for understanding or for completing a task.
- Interactive targets meet the 44x44px minimum, and meet it by being that size rather than by a side effect such as wrapped text.
- Screen readers are informed of state that changes without a page load: unread counts, send confirmations, and live updates.
- Contrast is verified against what actually renders in the browser, not against the token values a theme is assumed to apply. The theme layer has shipped a light card on a near-black page while the token file read as correct.

# Design System: LSAT Academy

## Overview

**Creative North Star: "LSAT Academy"**

One institution with two registers, and the seam between them is deliberate.

**The Hall** is the door. Unauthenticated, dark, and material: near-black ground, warm marble text, a bronze that carries the light, and a serif that behaves like carved lettering. Its own source calls it a museum nocturne, carved marble filmed in darkness, read by modern instruments. Its job is to make a prospective student feel they are entering somewhere, not signing up for something. It is the only place in the product where atmosphere outranks throughput.

**The Workroom** is everything past sign-in. Light, neutral, and quiet, because a student sits inside it for an hour reading dense argument text and the interface must not compete with the passage. There is no warmth here and that is the decision, not an oversight: warm accents need a dark ground to read, and on the Workroom's white they collapse. The Hall's bronze measures 8.58:1 on its own night and 2.30:1 on the Workroom's page. The two registers do not share a palette because they cannot.

What they do share is restraint about meaning. In both, colour is a signal. Green, red and amber say something happened. Nothing is coloured to be warm, to look premium, or to fill a space. Depth comes from hairlines and background tone rather than shadow, and hierarchy comes from size, weight and position rather than hue.

Inside the Workroom, individual rooms may commit fully to their own world. The eight Activity Centre worlds, the scoped bootcamps and the inbox each carry a painted identity behind a wrapper class. That is sanctioned and it is what an academy is: neutral corridors, characterful rooms. The corridor never takes on a room's paint.

**Key Characteristics:**
- Two registers, one institution: a dark marble Hall at the door, a light neutral Workroom inside.
- Colour means something or it is absent.
- Flat surfaces, hairline separation, no drop shadows.
- Precise and quiet controls that stay out of the reader's way.
- Rooms may be worlds; corridors never are.

## Colors

Two grounds, one shared vocabulary of meaning.

### Primary

- **Workroom Ink** (`#121212`): the Workroom's near-black. It is the text colour, the fill of every primary button, and the colour of the focus ring. The Workroom has no accent, so ink carries emphasis by itself.
- **Hall Bronze** (`#c6a467`): the Hall's warm light. Hairline borders on the call to action, a sheen across it on hover, and the selection highlight. It exists only on the Hall's night ground and never crosses into the Workroom.

### Secondary

- **Hall Cyan** (`#8ad8e2`): the Hall's cool instrument note against the bronze, and the colour of its focus outlines. Rare by design; it marks precision where bronze marks warmth.
- **Teacher Amber** (`#c7841f`): the single accent of the teacher workspace, with a hue-locked ramp for hover (`#af7318`), text on tinted fills (`#895910`) and a light step (`#e0a648`). Deliberately theme-independent: the workspace is always the light format. It is the one accent in the product, and it belongs to the operator, not the student.

### Tertiary

Individual rooms carry their own palettes behind a wrapper class and are documented in their own surface briefs, not here. The wrappers in use are `.mc-bootcamp`, `.ff-bootcamp`, `.rc-bootcamp`, `.topic-abstract`, `.cs-punk`, and the eight Activity Centre worlds.

### Neutral

- **Workroom Page** (`#fcfcfc`) and **Card** (`#ffffff`): the page ground and the surface that sits on it. One step of separation, no more.
- **Workroom Raised** (`#f7f7f7`) and **Fill** (`#f2f2f2`): the two tonal steps available for layering and for resting fills.
- **Workroom Ink Muted** (`#737373`): secondary text. Measures 4.62:1 on the page, which clears the floor with almost nothing to spare. Do not lighten it.
- **Workroom Hairline** (`#e0e0e0`) and **Field** (`#e6e6e6`): the borders that do the work shadows used to.
- **Hall Marble** (`#eae5da`), with secondary (`#a6a093`) and muted (`#6e695e`) steps: warm off-white text on the night ground.

### State (semantic only, never decorative)

- **Correct** (`#10b77f`) and **Incorrect** (`#f43e5c`): a student's attempt was right or wrong. Held under their own names, separate from system status, so a future shift to one cannot silently reframe the other.
- **Warn** (`#f59f0a`): a bonus, a stretch, a non-blocking notice.
- **Success** (`#21c45d`), **Warning** (`#f59f0a`), **Destructive** (`#dc2828`): system status, not student performance.

### Named Rules

**The Ground Decides Rule.** A warm colour is legible on the Hall's night and illegible on the Workroom's page. Never carry a colour across the seam on the strength of liking it; measure it against the ground it will land on first.

**The Meaning-Only Rule.** A colour appears in the Workroom because something is right, wrong, cautionary, or dangerous. There is no fifth reason. If a surface feels flat and the impulse is to add a colour, the answer is type and space.

**The Named Ground Rule.** No colour is written as a literal hex in a component. It resolves from a token, and a token that resolves to nothing is a defect, not a subtle effect. Three colour names in this codebase pointed at variables that were never defined, and the product silently lost its focus ring for five months.

## Typography

**Workroom Body:** Lexend (with Outfit, then the system stack)
**Hall Display:** Fraunces (with Iowan Old Style, Times New Roman, serif)
**Hall Inscription:** Cinzel (with Trajan Pro, serif)
**Mono:** IBM Plex Mono
**Question Lock:** Outfit, held deliberately for LSAT question surfaces

**Character:** The Workroom is one humanist sans doing everything, sized and weighted rather than swapped. The Hall is a serif institution: an optical serif for its headings and an inscriptional face for its few carved lines. The split is the whole typographic idea. Inside, type gets out of the way; at the door, type is the architecture.

### Hierarchy

- **Title** (600, 24px, line-height 1, tracking -0.025em): card and section headings in the Workroom.
- **Body** (400, 14px rising to 16px at 640px and above, line-height 1.6): everything a student reads in the Workroom chrome.
- **Question** (400, 16px, line-height 1.6, Outfit): LSAT stimulus and answer choices. Font-locked to Outfit even though the rest of the app moved to Lexend, so question text does not shift when the shell's face changes.
- **Label** (500, 14px): button and control labels.
- **Hall Display** (380, fluid, tracking -0.015em, Fraunces): Hall headings. The unusually light 380 weight is the point; it reads as carved rather than printed.
- **Hall Inscription** (500, tracking 0.05em, Cinzel): the Hall's few short carved lines. Never body copy.
- **Hall Mono** (400, 13px, tracking 0.04em, IBM Plex Mono): the Hall's instrument labels.

### Named Rules

**The One Face Rule.** The Workroom uses a single family. Hierarchy is built from size, weight and space. A second family inside the Workroom is a defect unless it is the Outfit question lock.

**The Question Never Moves Rule.** LSAT question text is font-locked. A change to the shell's typeface must not reflow a passage a student is mid-way through reading.

**The Serif Stays At The Door Rule.** Fraunces and Cinzel belong to the Hall. A serif appearing in the Workroom chrome means the seam has leaked.

## Layout

The Workroom is a centred single column with generous side gutters. The container centres by default, carries 32px of horizontal padding, and stops widening at 1400px.

Breakpoints are the framework defaults up to 1280px, with the largest step pulled in to 1400px. Body type steps from 14px to 16px at 640px, and form controls are forced to 16px below 768px so that iOS does not zoom the page when a field takes focus. Named breakpoints `phone` (up to 600px), `tablet` (601 to 900px) and `desktop` (901px and up) exist for bootcamp surfaces.

Spacing runs on a 4px base. The steps in real use are 4, 8, 16, 24 and 32. Card padding is 24px on all sides; button padding is 8px vertical and 16px horizontal.

Reading measure matters more here than in most products. Passage and stimulus text is held to a comfortable line length rather than allowed to run the full container width.

### Named Rules

**The Reading Measure Rule.** Any surface carrying an LSAT passage, stimulus, or explanation constrains its own line length. The container's maximum is a ceiling for layout, never a licence for prose.

## Elevation & Depth

**Flat, with hairline separation.** Surfaces sit on the page rather than above it. A card is distinguished from its ground by a one-pixel border and, where more is needed, by one step of background tone. There are no drop shadows in the Workroom.

This is a decision, not a description. The codebase currently ships five shadow tokens tuned for a dark ground, from `0 1px 3px rgba(0,0,0,0.4)` up to `0 8px 32px rgba(0,0,0,0.7)`, and applies the smallest of them to every card. Because the app ships light by default, those shadows land at 40 percent black under a white card. The light theme never redefined them, and the one glow token is white, so it is invisible on white. The tokens are retired by this record and the code has not caught up yet.

The Hall is the exception and keeps its own depth: fixed ambience gradients, a grain overlay, and an inset highlight on its call to action. That depth is atmospheric rather than structural, and it does not transfer.

### Named Rules

**The Flat Rule.** Depth in the Workroom comes from a hairline or a tonal step. A `box-shadow` on a Workroom surface is a defect.

**The Two-Step Rule.** Page, then card, then at most one raised step. A surface that needs a fourth level of layering is a surface that needs restructuring.

## Shapes

Corners are gently rounded and consistent: 8px for cards and containers, 6px for buttons and inputs, 4px for the smallest chips and insets, and a full pill only for genuine badges. The scale derives from one 0.5rem radius token, so a change to the base moves the whole system together.

The Hall uses a near-square 2px radius instead. Its edges are cut, not softened, which is what separates a carved surface from a card.

Borders are one pixel. There is no second border weight in the Workroom.

### Named Rules

**The Hairline Rule.** One pixel, always. A thicker border used as a coloured accent stripe on the leading edge of a surface is banned, including when faked with an inset shadow. Active state is carried by background tone.

## Components

### Buttons

- **Character:** precise and quiet. A button is a plain rectangle with a label. It darkens slightly on hover and does nothing else. It does not lift, does not cast a shadow, and does not scale on press.
- **Shape:** gently rounded (6px).
- **Primary:** ink fill (`#121212`), page-coloured label, 8px by 16px padding.
- **Outline:** page background, one-pixel field border, ink label.
- **Ghost:** no fill at rest, a tonal fill on hover.
- **Sizes:** default 44px tall, small 40px, large 48px, icon 44 by 44. Every size clears the 44px touch minimum by being that height, never by relying on wrapped text or padding accidents.
- **Focus:** a solid two-pixel ink outline, offset two pixels from the edge. This is the only focus treatment in the Workroom.
- **Hall CTA:** 60px tall, near-square corners, transparent fill over a bronze hairline, with a bronze sheen that sweeps across on hover and a cyan outline on focus.

### Cards / Containers

- **Corner:** 8px.
- **Background:** card white on the page ground.
- **Border:** one-pixel hairline. This is what separates the card, not a shadow.
- **Shadow:** none.
- **Padding:** 24px, with header, content and footer sharing that inset.

### Inputs / Fields

- **Style:** page background, one-pixel field border, 6px corners, 44px minimum height, 8px by 12px padding.
- **Focus:** the same two-pixel ink outline as buttons, offset two pixels. Do not express focus as a border colour change alone; a border shift is too quiet to serve as the only indicator.
- **Mobile:** 16px text below 768px, so focusing a field never zooms the viewport.

### Navigation

Neutral chrome throughout. The sidebar runs one tonal step off the page ground with a hairline edge, and the active item is marked by background tone rather than by a coloured rule on its leading edge.

### Signature Component: the scoped world

The product's distinctive pattern is not a control, it is a container. A room commits fully to its own palette, typefaces and atmosphere behind a single wrapper class, and nothing inside it leaks out. Scene layers are `aria-hidden` furniture painted beneath the content, so the accessible tree is identical whether the room is painted or bare. A new world is added by writing a new wrapper and its prefixed tokens, never by adding hex to a shared component.

## Do's and Don'ts

### Do:

- **Do** resolve every colour from a token, and treat a token that resolves to nothing as a defect. Verify a new token renders before documenting it.
- **Do** give every interactive element a visible focus indicator: a solid two-pixel ink outline offset two pixels in the Workroom, cyan in the Hall.
- **Do** check contrast against the ground the colour will actually land on, in the theme that actually ships, in a browser. The theme file has read as correct while the page rendered a light card on a near-black ground.
- **Do** separate surfaces with a one-pixel hairline and one tonal step.
- **Do** keep colour semantic: correct, incorrect, warning, danger. Nothing else earns it.
- **Do** scope a new room's identity behind its own wrapper class with prefixed tokens, the way the bootcamps and the eight Activity worlds already do.
- **Do** hold LSAT passage and stimulus text to a readable line length, and leave it on the Outfit question lock.
- **Do** end every world's stylesheet with a `prefers-reduced-motion` block that stops continuous motion rather than speeding it up.

### Don't:

- **Don't** add a `box-shadow` to a Workroom surface. Depth is hairline and tone.
- **Don't** carry a Hall colour into the Workroom. Bronze reads 8.58:1 on the Hall's night and 2.30:1 on the Workroom's page.
- **Don't** introduce a warm or metallic accent into the Workroom to make it feel premium. Premium here is depth, hierarchy and type.
- **Don't** remove the browser's focus outline without shipping a replacement in the same rule. `outline-none` with no working ring is worse than leaving the default alone.
- **Don't** animate the focus indicator. A `transition-all` on a control means the ring fades in, which delays the one signal a keyboard user depends on.
- **Don't** use a border thicker than one pixel as a coloured accent stripe, including when faked with an inset shadow.
- **Don't** put a second typeface into the Workroom chrome, and don't let a serif cross in from the Hall.
- **Don't** animate layout properties. Animate `opacity`, `transform` and `color`. No bounce, no elastic, no lift on hover.
- **Don't** write a hex value into a component. If a room needs its own colour, promote it to named tokens behind that room's wrapper.

   the previous DESIGN.md as a brand accent it never was. All three names and the
   bronze-literal `glow-sm` shadow are removed, and their thirteen call sites now use
   neutral tokens. Three of those call sites were visibly broken, not merely untokenized:
   the journal ReviewForm's Next button rendered near-white text on a transparent
   background, its active step dot was invisible while the inactive dots showed, and the
   Annotate toggle's active state was indistinguishable from its inactive state.
   `docs/chart-palette.md` and `scripts/validate-chart-palette.mjs` still carry the
   inherited hex and have not been touched.

3. Shadow tokens are dark-tuned and the app ships light. `shadow-sm` puts
   rgba(0,0,0,0.4) under a white card. Retired above; still applied by `ui/card.tsx`.

4. Contrast failures in the shipped light theme, measured: `--text-tertiary` 3.28:1,
   `--success` 2.24:1, `--warning` 2.08:1, `--state-correct` 2.53:1,
   `--state-incorrect` 3.60:1, `--teacher-accent` 3.03:1 as text. In the dark
   alternate, `--text-tertiary` 4.18:1. AA needs 4.5:1 for normal text.

5. Components are tactile today, not quiet. Buttons lift, scale on press, and gain
   a shadow on hover; cards carry `shadow-sm`. The record above chooses quiet and flat.

## user

Tool result:
Run systematic **technical** quality checks and generate a comprehensive report. Don't fix issues — document them for other commands to address.

This is a code-level audit, not a design critique. Check what's measurable and verifiable in the implementation.

## Diagnostic Scan

Run comprehensive checks across 5 dimensions. Score each dimension 0-4 using the criteria below.

### 1. Accessibility (A11y)

**Check for**:
- **Contrast issues**: Text contrast ratios < 4.5:1 (or 7:1 for AAA)
- **Missing ARIA**: Interactive elements without proper roles, labels, or states
- **Keyboard navigation**: Missing focus indicators, illogical tab order, keyboard traps
- **Semantic HTML**: Improper heading hierarchy, missing landmarks, divs instead of buttons
- **Alt text**: Missing or poor image descriptions
- **Form issues**: Inputs without labels, poor error messaging, missing required indicators

**Score 0-4**: 0=Inaccessible (fails WCAG A), 1=Major gaps (few ARIA labels, no keyboard nav), 2=Partial (some a11y effort, significant gaps), 3=Good (WCAG AA mostly met, minor gaps), 4=Excellent (WCAG AA fully met, approaches AAA)

### 2. Performance

**Check for**:
- **Layout thrashing**: Reading/writing layout properties in loops
- **Expensive animations**: Casual layout-property animation, unbounded blur/filter/shadow effects, or effects that visibly drop frames
- **Missing optimization**: Images without lazy loading, unoptimized assets, missing will-change
- **Bundle size**: Unnecessary imports, unused dependencies
- **Render performance**: Unnecessary re-renders, missing memoization

**Score 0-4**: 0=Severe issues (layout thrash, unoptimized everything), 1=Major problems (no lazy loading, expensive animations), 2=Partial (some optimization, gaps remain), 3=Good (mostly optimized, minor improvements possible), 4=Excellent (fast, lean, well-optimized)

### 3. Theming

**Check for**:
- **Hard-coded colors**: Colors not using design tokens
- **Broken dark mode**: Missing dark mode variants, poor contrast in dark theme
- **Inconsistent tokens**: Using wrong tokens, mixing token types
- **Theme switching issues**: Values that don't update on theme change

**Score 0-4**: 0=No theming (hard-coded everything), 1=Minimal tokens (mostly hard-coded), 2=Partial (tokens exist but inconsistently used), 3=Good (tokens used, minor hard-coded values), 4=Excellent (full token system, dark mode works perfectly)

### 4. Responsive Design

**Check for**:
- **Fixed widths**: Hard-coded widths that break on mobile
- **Touch targets**: Interactive elements < 44x44px
- **Horizontal scroll**: Content overflow on narrow viewports
- **Text scaling**: Layouts that break when text size increases
- **Missing breakpoints**: No mobile/tablet variants

**Score 0-4**: 0=Desktop-only (breaks on mobile), 1=Major issues (some breakpoints, many failures), 2=Partial (works on mobile, rough edges), 3=Good (responsive, minor touch target or overflow issues), 4=Excellent (fluid, all viewports, proper touch targets)

### 5. Anti-Patterns (CRITICAL)

Check against ALL the **DON'T** guidelines from the parent impeccable skill (already loaded in this context). Look for AI slop tells (AI color palette, gradient text, glassmorphism, hero metrics, card grids, generic fonts) and general design anti-patterns (gray on color, nested cards, bounce easing, redundant copy).

**Score 0-4**: 0=AI slop gallery (5+ tells), 1=Heavy AI aesthetic (3-4 tells), 2=Some tells (1-2 noticeable), 3=Mostly clean (subtle issues only), 4=No AI tells (distinctive, intentional design)

## Generate Report

### Audit Health Score

| # | Dimension | Score | Key Finding |
|---|-----------|-------|-------------|
| 1 | Accessibility | ? | [most critical a11y issue or "--"] |
| 2 | Performance | ? | |
| 3 | Responsive Design | ? | |
| 4 | Theming | ? | |
| 5 | Anti-Patterns | ? | |
| **Total** | | **??/20** | **[Rating band]** |

**Rating bands**: 18-20 Excellent (minor polish), 14-17 Good (address weak dimensions), 10-13 Acceptable (significant work needed), 6-9 Poor (major overhaul), 0-5 Critical (fundamental issues)

### Anti-Patterns Verdict
**Start here.** Pass/fail: Does this look AI-generated? List specific tells. Be brutally honest.

### Executive Summary
- Audit Health Score: **??/20** ([rating band])
- Total issues found (count by severity: P0/P1/P2/P3)
- Top 3-5 critical issues
- Recommended next steps

### Detailed Findings by Severity

Tag every issue with **P0-P3 severity**:
- **P0 Blocking**: Prevents task completion — fix immediately
- **P1 Major**: Significant difficulty or WCAG AA violation — fix before release
- **P2 Minor**: Annoyance, workaround exists — fix in next pass
- **P3 Polish**: Nice-to-fix, no real user impact — fix if time permits

For each issue, document:
- **[P?] Issue name**
- **Location**: Component, file, line
- **Category**: Accessibility / Performance / Theming / Responsive / Anti-Pattern
- **Impact**: How it affects users
- **WCAG/Standard**: Which standard it violates (if applicable)
- **Recommendation**: How to fix it
- **Suggested command**: Which command to use (prefer: $impeccable adapt, $impeccable animate, $impeccable audit, $impeccable bolder, $impeccable clarify, $impeccable colorize, $impeccable critique, $impeccable delight, $impeccable distill, $impeccable document, $impeccable harden, $impeccable layout, $impeccable onboard, $impeccable optimize, $impeccable overdrive, $impeccable polish, $impeccable quieter, $impeccable shape, $impeccable typeset)

### Patterns & Systemic Issues

Identify recurring problems that indicate systemic gaps rather than one-off mistakes:
- "Hard-coded colors appear in 15+ components, should use design tokens"
- "Touch targets consistently too small (<44px) throughout mobile experience"

### Positive Findings

Note what's working well — good practices to maintain and replicate.

## Recommended Actions

List recommended commands in priority order (P0 first, then P1, then P2):

1. **[P?] `$command-name`** — Brief description (specific context from audit findings)
2. **[P?] `$command-name`** — Brief description (specific context)

**Rules**: Only recommend commands from: $impeccable adapt, $impeccable animate, $impeccable audit, $impeccable bolder, $impeccable clarify, $impeccable colorize, $impeccable critique, $impeccable delight, $impeccable distill, $impeccable document, $impeccable harden, $impeccable layout, $impeccable onboard, $impeccable optimize, $impeccable overdrive, $impeccable polish, $impeccable quieter, $impeccable shape, $impeccable typeset. Map findings to the most appropriate command. End with `$impeccable polish` as the final step if any fixes were recommended.

After presenting the summary, tell the user:

> You can ask me to run these one at a time, all at once, or in any order you prefer.
>
> Re-run `$impeccable audit` after fixes to see your score improve.

**IMPORTANT**: Be thorough but actionable. Too many P3 issues creates noise. Focus on what actually matters.

**NEVER**:
- Report issues without explaining impact (why does this matter?)
- Provide generic recommendations (be specific and actionable)
- Skip positive findings (celebrate what works)
- Forget to prioritize (everything can't be P0)
- Report false positives without verification

Remember: You're a technical quality auditor. Document systematically, prioritize ruthlessly, cite specific code locations, and provide clear paths to improvement.

    85	export function ClassroomItemView() {
    86	  const { itemId } = useParams<{ itemId: string }>();
    87	  const navigate = useNavigate();
    88	  const query = useClassroomItems();
    89	  const item = React.useMemo(
    90	    () => (query.data ?? []).find((candidate) => candidate.id === itemId) ?? null,
    91	    [itemId, query.data],
    92	  );
    93	
    94	  const queryClient = useQueryClient();
    95	
    96	  // Opening a note marks it read and clears the bell entry that pointed at it.
    97	  // Deliberately not gated on status: the status update is guarded on
    98	  // 'assigned' inside markNoteRead so it is a no-op on a second visit, while
    99	  // the notification clear still has to run, because a note can be read on one
   100	  // device and leave its bell entry standing on another.
   101	  React.useEffect(() => {
   102	    if (!item || !NOTE_TYPES.has(item.type)) return;
   103	    let cancelled = false;
   104	    void markNoteRead(item)
   105	      .then(() => {
   106	        if (!cancelled) void queryClient.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ["classroom-items"] });
   107	      })
   108	      .catch(() => {
   109	        // Read state is a convenience. A failure here must never block the note.
   110	      });
   111	    return () => {
   112	      cancelled = true;
   113	    };
   114	  }, [item, queryClient]);
   115	
   116	  React.useEffect(() => {
   117	    if (!item) return;
   118	    if (
   119	      item.type === "drill" ||
   120	      item.type === "homework" ||
   121	      item.type === "activity" ||
   122	      item.type === "bootcamp_module" ||
   123	      item.type === "weekly_plan_task"
   124	    ) {
   125	      const route = resolveItemRoute(item);
   126	      if (route) navigate(route, { replace: true });
   127	    }
   128	  }, [item, navigate]);
   129	
   130	  const safeHtml = React.useMemo(
   131	    () => sanitizeClassroomHtml(item?.content_html ?? ""),
   132	    [item?.content_html],
   133	  );
   134	
   135	  if (query.isLoading && !query.data) return <DetailLoading />;
   136	  if (query.error) {
   137	    return (
   138	      <DetailError
   139	        message="Could not load this classroom item."
   140	        retry={() => void query.refetch()}
   141	      />
   142	    );
   143	  }
   144	  if (!item) {
   145	    return (
   146	      <>
   147	        <DetailHeading
   148	          date={new Date().toISOString()}
   149	          kind="Classroom"

   154	          className="classroom-button"
   155	          onClick={() => navigate("/classroom")}
   156	        >
   157	          Back to your room
   158	        </button>
   159	      </>
   160	    );
   161	  }
   162	
   163	  return (
   164	    <>
   165	      <DetailHeading

   188	export function ClassroomRecapsView() {
   189	  const query = useClassroomItems();
   190	  const { unread, read } = groupNotes(query.data ?? []);
   191	  const total = unread.length + read.length;
   192	
   193	  const row = (note: (typeof unread)[number], isUnread: boolean) => ({
   194	    key: note.id,
   195	    title: note.title,
   196	    meta: shortDate(note.created_at),
   197	    route: `/classroom/item/${encodeURIComponent(note.id)}`,
   198	    unread: isUnread,
   199	  });
   200	
   201	  return (
   202	    <>
   203	      <DetailHeading
   204	        date={new Date().toISOString()}
   205	        kind="Classroom"
   206	        title="Notes and recaps"
   207	      />
   208	      {query.isLoading && !query.data ? <DetailLoading /> : null}
   209	      {query.error ? (
   210	        <DetailError
   211	          message="Could not load your notes and recaps."
   212	          retry={() => void query.refetch()}
   213	        />
   214	      ) : null}
   215	      {!query.isLoading && !query.error && total > 0 ? (
   216	        <>
   217	          <StageIndexHint>
   218	            {unread.length > 0
   219	              ? `${unread.length} unread of ${total}.`
   220	              : `All ${total} read.`}
   221	          </StageIndexHint>
   222	          {unread.length > 0 ? (
   223	            <StageIndexList
   224	              label="Unread notes and recaps"
   225	              heading="Unread"
   226	              items={unread.map((note) => row(note, true))}
   227	            />
   228	          ) : null}
   229	          {read.length > 0 ? (
   230	            <StageIndexList
   231	              label="Notes and recaps you have read"
   232	              heading={unread.length > 0 ? "Read" : undefined}
   233	              items={read.map((note) => row(note, false))}
   234	            />
   235	          ) : null}
   236	        </>
   237	      ) : null}
   238	    </>
   239	  );
   240	}

    18	 * route was telling the student to "choose an entry from the list" while no
    19	 * list was anywhere on screen — which covers a 13" laptop and any window with
    20	 * a panel docked.
    21	 *
    22	 * Both copies are always rendered and CSS shows exactly one, so there is no
    23	 * width measured in JS and nothing to re-run on resize. `display: none` also
    24	 * keeps the hidden copy out of the accessibility tree, so it is not read twice.
    25	 */

.classroom-root .classroom-section-heading button {
  align-items: center;
  background: transparent;
  border: 0;
  cursor: pointer;
  display: flex;
  justify-content: center;
  margin-left: auto;
  padding: 4px;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-panel-list {
  display: grid;
  gap: 4px;
  margin: 0 -8px;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-panel-item {
  background: transparent;
  border: 0;
  border-radius: var(--classroom-radius);
  cursor: pointer;
  padding: 8px;
  text-align: left;
  transition: background-color var(--classroom-transition) ease;
  width: 100%;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-panel-item:hover {
  background: var(--classroom-hover);
}

.classroom-root .classroom-panel-item-active {
  background: var(--classroom-active);
}

.classroom-root .classroom-panel-item h3 {
  color: var(--classroom-ink);
  font-size: 14px;
  font-weight: 500;
  line-height: 1.45;
  margin: 0 0 4px;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-panel-item time {
  color: var(--classroom-meta-soft);
  font-size: 0.75rem;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-panel-item-active h3,
.classroom-root .classroom-panel-item-active time {
  color: var(--classroom-active-ink);
}

.classroom-root .classroom-panel-item-active time {
  opacity: 0.6;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-list-state {
  display: grid;
  gap: 10px;
  padding: 4px 0;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-list-state span,
.classroom-root .classroom-skeleton {
  background: var(--classroom-line);
  border-radius: 4px;
  display: block;
  height: 13px;
  opacity: 0.75;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-list-state span:nth-child(2) {
  width: 72%;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-list-state span:nth-child(3) {
  width: 84%;
  border-radius: var(--classroom-radius);
  cursor: pointer;
  display: none;
  font-size: 14px;
  height: 48px;
  justify-content: space-between;
  margin-bottom: 32px;
  padding: 0 16px;
  width: 100%;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-dateline {
  align-items: center;
  color: var(--classroom-meta-soft);
  display: flex;
  font-size: 14px;
  font-weight: 500;
  gap: 6px;
  margin-bottom: 16px;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-title {
  font-size: 30px;
  font-weight: 700;
  letter-spacing: -0.02em;
  line-height: 1.15;
  margin: 0 0 24px;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-message {
  border: 1px solid var(--classroom-line);
  border-radius: var(--classroom-radius);
  margin-bottom: 48px;
  padding: 20px;
  transition: border-color var(--classroom-transition) ease;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-message header {
  align-items: center;
  display: flex;
  gap: 10px;
  display: flex;
  flex-wrap: wrap;
  gap: 8px;
  margin-top: 16px;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-button {
  background: transparent;
  border: 1px solid var(--classroom-line);
  border-radius: var(--classroom-radius);
  cursor: pointer;
  font-family: "Outfit", ui-sans-serif, sans-serif;
  font-size: 13px;
  font-weight: 500;
  padding: 8px 14px;
  transition:
    background-color var(--classroom-transition) ease,
    border-color var(--classroom-transition) ease;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-button:hover {
  background: var(--classroom-hover);
}

.classroom-root .classroom-button-primary {
  background: var(--classroom-link);
  border-color: var(--classroom-link);
  color: #ffffff;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-button-primary:hover {
  background: var(--classroom-link);
}

.classroom-root .classroom-content-section {
  margin-bottom: 48px;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-section-header {
  align-items: baseline;
  display: flex;
  position: absolute;
  width: 100%;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-inline-error {
  align-items: center;
  border: 1px solid var(--classroom-line);
  border-radius: var(--classroom-radius);
  display: flex;
  gap: 12px;
  padding: 12px 14px;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-inline-error p {
  font-size: 14px;
  margin: 0;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-inline-error .classroom-button {
  margin-left: auto;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-skeleton-stack {
  display: grid;
  gap: 10px;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-skeleton-stack .classroom-skeleton:nth-child(2) {
  width: 62%;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-skeleton-stack .classroom-skeleton:nth-child(3) {
  width: 78%;
}

.classroom-root .classroom-note-meta {
  border: 1px dashed var(--classroom-line);
  border-radius: var(--classroom-radius);
  color: rgb(var(--classroom-meta));
  line-height: 1.7;
  margin-bottom: 32px;
  top: 0;
  width: min(100%, 420px);
}

.classroom-root .classroom-section-overlay .classroom-section-card {
  border-left: 1px solid var(--classroom-line);
  border-radius: var(--classroom-radius) 0 0 var(--classroom-radius);
}

.classroom-root .classroom-empty-copy {
  color: var(--classroom-meta-soft);
  font-size: 15px;
  line-height: 1.65;
}

/* ---- Flagged ---- */

.classroom-root .classroom-panel-search {
  background: transparent;
  border: 1px solid var(--classroom-line);
  border-radius: var(--classroom-radius);
  color: var(--classroom-ink);
  font-family: inherit;
  font-size: 13px;
  margin-bottom: 12px;
  padding: 7px 10px;
  gap: 8px;
  margin-top: 16px;
}

/* --------------------------------------------------------------------------
   Stage-side index list (H5)

   The section list column is hidden below 1280px, and below 1400px for Wrong
   answers, where it collapses behind a trigger. At those widths the index
   routes were telling the student to choose from a list that was not on
   screen. Both copies render; these rules show exactly one.
   -------------------------------------------------------------------------- */

.classroom-root .classroom-stage-index {
  display: none;
  gap: 4px;
  margin: 0 -8px;
}

@media (max-width: 1279px) {
  .classroom-root .classroom-stage-index {
    display: grid;
  }

  .classroom-root .classroom-stage-index-hint {
    display: none;
  }
}

/* Wrong answers keeps its column 120px longer than the rest, so it swaps over
   at its own breakpoint rather than the shared one. */
@media (max-width: 1399px) {
  .classroom-root.classroom-root-journal .classroom-stage-index {
    display: grid;
  }

  .classroom-root.classroom-root-journal .classroom-stage-index-hint {
    display: none;
  }
}

/* ---- Unread notes -------------------------------------------------------
   A note is unread until it is opened (classroom_items.status === 'assigned').
   Two markers, both quiet: a dot on the row, and a count beside the panel
   title. Colour alone never carries the state — the dot has a size and a
   position, the count has a number, and both rows also carry an sr-only
   "(unread)" so this is not a colour-only signal. */

.classroom-unread-dot {
  display: inline-block;
  width: 0.45rem;
  height: 0.45rem;
  margin-right: 0.45rem;
  border-radius: 50%;
  background: var(--classroom-accent, currentColor);
  vertical-align: middle;
  flex: 0 0 auto;
}

.classroom-unread-count {
  display: inline-flex;
  align-items: center;
  justify-content: center;
  min-width: 1.25rem;
  height: 1.25rem;
  margin-left: 0.5rem;
  padding: 0 0.35rem;
  border-radius: 999px;
  background: var(--classroom-accent, currentColor);
  color: var(--classroom-surface, #fff);
  font-size: 0.6875rem;
  font-weight: 650;
  font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums;
  vertical-align: middle;
}

/* Group label above a list, used when Notes and recaps splits unread from
   read. Only rendered when there is more than one list on the page. */
.classroom-stage-index-heading {
  margin: 1.4rem 0 0.5rem;
  font-size: 0.6875rem;
  font-weight: 650;
  letter-spacing: 0.12em;
  text-transform: uppercase;
  opacity: 0.55;
}
.classroom-stage-index-heading:first-child {
  margin-top: 0;
}


/* ── Admissions ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   The 509 numbers for every accredited school, and where the student's own
   score sits against each class. A table, deliberately: the student is
   comparing rows, and anything prettier would make that harder. */

.classroom-root .classroom-admissions-head {
  align-items: flex-end;
  display: flex;
  flex-wrap: wrap;
  gap: 16px;

    !homeworkQuery.error,

  // Unread first, then read, each newest first. Same ordering as the Notes and

  const noteRows = React.useMemo(() => {

  const { rows: feedRows } = useAssignedTodoFeed();

  const openRow = (route: string) => {

  };

  return (

            {section === "notes" && unreadNotes > 0 ? (

          {onClose ? (

              <X size={16} />

function JournalSectionList({
  activeId,
  onClose,
}: {
  activeId: string | undefined;
  onClose?: () => void;
}) {
  const navigate = useNavigate();
  const query = useJournalEntries();
  const [filter, setFilter] = React.useState("");
  const rows = React.useMemo(() => {
    const source = query.data ?? [];
    const needle = filter.trim().toLowerCase();
    if (!needle) return source;
    return source.filter(
      (row) =>
        row.label.toLowerCase().includes(needle) ||
        row.qid.toLowerCase().includes(needle),
    );
  }, [filter, query.data]);

  const openRow = (qid: string) => {
    onClose?.();
    navigate(`/journal/${encodeURIComponent(qid)}`);
  };

  return (
          type="button"
          className="classroom-mobile-menu-button"
          onClick={() => setMobileMenuOpen(true)}
          aria-label="Open classroom menu"
        >
          <Menu size={19} />
        </button>

        <aside className="classroom-sidebar">
          <ClassroomSidebar
            displayName={sidebarName}
            activeSection={activeSection}
            onSection={openSection}
            onHome={goHome}
          />
        </aside>

        {mobileMenuOpen ? (
          <div className="classroom-mobile-menu">
            <button
              type="button"
              className="classroom-overlay-backdrop"
              onClick={() => setMobileMenuOpen(false)}
              aria-label="Close classroom menu"
            />
            <aside>
              <button
                type="button"
                className="classroom-overlay-close"
                onClick={() => setMobileMenuOpen(false)}
                aria-label="Close classroom menu"
              >
                <X size={18} />
              </button>
              <ClassroomSidebar
                displayName={sidebarName}
                activeSection={activeSection}
                onSection={(section) => {
                  setMobileMenuOpen(false);
                  openSection(section);
                }}
                onHome={goHome}
                onNavigate={() => setMobileMenuOpen(false)}
              />
            </aside>
          </div>
        ) : null}

        {activeSection ? (
          <aside className="classroom-section-list">
            <SectionList section={activeSection} activeId={activeId} />
          </aside>
        ) : null}

        {journalDetail ? (
          <React.Suspense fallback={<StageFallback />}>
            <Outlet context={contextValue} />
          </React.Suspense>
        ) : messagesStage ? (
          <main className="classroom-messages-stage">
            <React.Suspense fallback={<StageFallback />}>
              <Outlet context={contextValue} />
            </React.Suspense>
          </main>
        ) : workspaceStage ? (
          <main className="classroom-workspace-stage">
            <React.Suspense fallback={<StageFallback />}>
              <Outlet context={contextValue} />
            </React.Suspense>
          </main>
        ) : (
          <main className="classroom-stage">
            <div className="classroom-column">
              {activeSection ? (
                // The stage already carries the surface's name as its h1, so
                // printing it here too named the same thing twice. The button
                // says what it does; the section name stays for screen
                // readers, which have no h1 next to it for context.
                <button
                  type="button"
                  className="classroom-section-trigger"
                  aria-label={`Open the ${SECTION_TITLES[activeSection].toLowerCase()} list`}
                  onClick={() => setSectionOverlayOpen(true)}
                >
                  Open the list
                  <PanelLeftClose size={17} />
                </button>
              ) : null}
              <React.Suspense fallback={<StageFallback />}>
                <Outlet context={contextValue} />
              </React.Suspense>
            </div>
          </main>
        )}

        {activeSection && sectionOverlayOpen ? (
          <div className="classroom-section-overlay">
            <button
              type="button"
              className="classroom-overlay-backdrop"
              onClick={() => setSectionOverlayOpen(false)}
              aria-label="Close section list"
            />
            <aside>
              <SectionList
                section={activeSection}
                activeId={activeId}
                onClose={() => setSectionOverlayOpen(false)}
              />
            </aside>
          </div>
        ) : null}
      </div>
    </ClassroomLayoutContext.Provider>
  );
}

  private async mergeExplanationSidecars(): Promise<void> {
    const pts = new Set<number>();
    for (const q of this.questions.values()) pts.add(q.pt);
    const results = await Promise.all(
      Array.from(pts).map(async (pt) => {
        try {
          const res = await fetch(`/data/explanations/PT${pt}-explanations.json`);
          if (!res.ok) return null;
          const json = await res.json();
          return json && typeof json === 'object' ? (json as Record<string, LRExplanation>) : null;
        } catch {
          return null;
        }
      }),
    );
    let attached = 0;
    for (const sidecar of results) {
      if (!sidecar) continue;
      for (const [qid, expl] of Object.entries(sidecar)) {
        const q = this.questions.get(qid);
        if (!q) continue;
        q.explanations = expl;
        if (!q.answerChoiceExplanations) {
          q.answerChoiceExplanations = this.deriveTypedExplanations(expl, q.correctAnswer);
        }
        attached += 1;
      }
    }
    if (attached > 0) {
      console.log(`Question bank: attached LSAT Lab explanations to ${attached} question(s).`);
    }
  }

  private deriveTypedExplanations(
    expl: LRExplanation,
    correctAnswer: LRQuestion['correctAnswer'],
  ): LRQuestion['answerChoiceExplanations'] | undefined {
    const style = expl.styles?.standard ?? expl.styles?.brief ?? expl.styles?.anotherWay;
    if (!style) return undefined;
    const out: any = {};
    for (const L of ['A', 'B', 'C', 'D', 'E'] as const) {
      const c = style.choices?.[L];
      const isCorrect = L === (expl.correctChoice ?? correctAnswer);

function routeNeedsQuestionBank(pathname: string): boolean {
  return !BANK_FREE_PREFIXES.some(
    (prefix) => pathname === prefix || pathname.startsWith(`${prefix}/`),
  );
}

export function QuestionBankProvider({ children }: { children: ReactNode }) {
  const { pathname } = useLocation();

  const [manifest, setManifest] = useState<QuestionManifest | null>(() =>
    questionBank.isReady() ? questionBank.getManifest() : null,
  );
  // Must start true on a route that will load, or the first render hands consumers
  // isLoading=false with an empty bank and they flash "not found" before the fetch
  // lands. Consumers gate their direct questionBank.getQuestion() calls on this.
  const [isLoading, setIsLoading] = useState<boolean>(
    () => !questionBank.isReady() && routeNeedsQuestionBank(pathname),
  );
  const [error, setError] = useState<string | null>(null);

  useEffect(() => {
    if (questionBank.isReady()) {
      setManifest(questionBank.getManifest());
      setIsLoading(false);
      return;
    }

    // Re-evaluated on every navigation, so entering a consumer route from a
    // bank-free one still loads. questionBank.load() dedupes concurrent callers
    // and returns immediately once ready, so repeat calls are free.
    if (!routeNeedsQuestionBank(pathname)) {
      setIsLoading(false);
      return;
    }

    let cancelled = false;
    setIsLoading(true);

    questionBank
      .load()
      .then(() => {
        if (cancelled) return;
        setManifest(questionBank.getManifest());
        setIsLoading(false);
      })
      .catch((err) => {
        if (cancelled) return;
        console.error('Failed to load question bank:', err);
        setError('Failed to load questions');
        setIsLoading(false);
      });

    return () => {
      cancelled = true;
    };
  }, [pathname]);

  return (
    <QuestionBankContext.Provider value={{ manifest, isLoading, error }}>
      {children}
    </QuestionBankContext.Provider>

import { createContext, useContext, useEffect, useState, type ReactNode } from "react";
import { useLocation } from "react-router-dom";
import { questionBank, QuestionManifest } from '@/lib/questionLoader';

interface QuestionBankContextType {
  manifest: QuestionManifest | null;
  isLoading: boolean;
  error: string | null;
}

const QuestionBankContext = createContext<QuestionBankContextType | undefined>(undefined);

/**
 * Routes that provably never read the question bank, verified by grep: nothing
 * under src/features/activity-centre/, src/pages/Auth.tsx, or src/features/rc/
 * imports questionLoader or useQuestionBank. Every /rc route — player, results,
 * search, redirect — renders out of src/features/rc/ and reads the RC bank in
 * public/data/rc/ instead, so a student opening one passage was downloading the
 * whole Logical Reasoning bank and firing an explanation request per PrepTest
 * before the passage could paint.
 *
 * This is a DENYLIST on purpose. The bank is ~5.5MB raw (1.4MB gzipped) and costs
 * ~111ms of blocked main thread plus 9-14 explanation fetches, and the provider sits
 * on the catch-all route (App.tsx), so every page paid for it — including /auth,
 * before the student had even signed in.
 *
 * An allowlist would be the tidier shape and is the eventual fix (a provider per
 * consumer route group). It is not safe to write yet: 24 modules read the
 * `questionBank` singleton directly without subscribing to this context, so
 * "does this route call useQuestionBank()" is not a reliable test for "does this
 * route need the bank" — /analytics is the clearest example. Getting an allowlist
 * wrong renders a page with silently empty questions; getting this denylist wrong
 * is impossible for any route not named in it, because every other route keeps
 * exactly the behaviour it had before.
 *
 * Add to this list only after grepping the route's whole subtree for questionLoader.
 */
const BANK_FREE_PREFIXES = ['/activities', '/activity-lab', '/auth', '/rc'] as const;

Tool result:
> **Additional context needed**: what the interface is trying to accomplish.

### Gather Assessments

Launch two independent assessments. **Neither may see the other's output** — this isolation is what makes the combined score honest. Running both in one head silently anchors them to each other; do not shortcut it for cost, speed, or context-size reasons.

Delegate each assessment to a separate sub-agent (Claude Code's `Agent` tool, Codex's subagent spawning, etc.). Each returns structured findings as text. Do NOT output findings to the user yet.

Fall back to sequential in-head work only if the environment genuinely cannot spawn sub-agents.

**Tab isolation**: When browser automation is available, each assessment MUST create its own new tab. Never reuse an existing tab, even if one is already open at the correct URL. This prevents the two assessments from interfering with each other's page state.

#### Assessment A: LLM Design Review

Read the relevant source files (HTML, CSS, JS/TS) and, if browser automation is available, visually inspect the live page. **Create a new tab** for this; do not reuse existing tabs. After navigation, label the tab by setting the document title:
```javascript
document.title = '[LLM] ' + document.title;
```
Think like a design director. Evaluate:

**AI Slop Detection (CRITICAL)**: Does this look like every other AI-generated interface? Review against ALL **DON'T** guidelines from the parent impeccable skill (already loaded in this context). Check for AI color palette, gradient text, dark glows, glassmorphism, hero metric layouts, identical card grids, generic fonts, and all other tells. **The test**: If someone said "AI made this," would you believe them immediately?

**Holistic Design Review**: visual hierarchy (eye flow, primary action clarity), information architecture (structure, grouping, cognitive load), emotional resonance (does it match brand and audience?), discoverability (are interactive elements obvious?), composition (balance, whitespace, rhythm), typography (hierarchy, readability, font choices), color (purposeful use, cohesion, accessibility), states & edge cases (empty, loading, error, success), microcopy (clarity, tone, helpfulness).

**Cognitive Load** (consult [cognitive-load](cognitive-load.md)):
- Run the 8-item cognitive load checklist. Report failure count: 0-1 = low (good), 2-3 = moderate, 4+ = critical.
- Count visible options at each decision point. If >4, flag it.
- Check for progressive disclosure: is complexity revealed only when needed?

**Emotional Journey**:
- What emotion does this interface evoke? Is that intentional?
- **Peak-end rule**: Is the most intense moment positive? Does the experience end well?
- **Emotional valleys**: Check for anxiety spikes at high-stakes moments (payment, delete, commit). Are there design interventions (progress indicators, reassurance copy, undo options)?

**Nielsen's Heuristics** (consult [heuristics-scoring](heuristics-scoring.md)):
Score each of the 10 heuristics 0-4. This scoring will be presented in the report.

Return structured findings covering: AI slop verdict, heuristic scores, cognitive load assessment, what's working (2-3 items), priority issues (3-5 with what/why/fix), minor observations, and provocative questions.

#### Assessment B: Automated Detection

Run the bundled deterministic detector, which flags 25 specific patterns (AI slop tells + general design quality).

**CLI scan**:
```bash
npx impeccable --json [--fast] [target]
```

- Pass HTML/JSX/TSX/Vue/Svelte files or directories as `[target]` (anything with markup). Do not pass CSS-only files.
- For URLs, skip the CLI scan (it requires Puppeteer). Use browser visualization instead.
- For large directories (200+ scannable files), use `--fast` (regex-only, skips jsdom)
- For 500+ files, narrow scope or ask the user
- Exit code 0 = clean, 2 = findings

**Browser visualization** — **required** when browser automation tools are available AND the target is a viewable page. The `[Human]` overlay tab is the user-facing deliverable; the critique is incomplete without it. Skip only if the target is not a viewable page (CSS-only file, non-browser target).

The overlay is a **visual aid for the user**. It highlights issues directly in their browser. Do NOT scroll through the page to screenshot overlays. Instead, read the console output to get the results programmatically.

1. **Start the live detection server**:
   ```bash
   npx impeccable live &
   ```
   Note the port printed to stdout (auto-assigned). Use `--port=PORT` to fix it.
2. **Create a new tab** and navigate to the page (use dev server URL for local files, or direct URL). Do not reuse existing tabs.
3. **Label the tab** via `javascript_tool` so the user can distinguish it:
   ```javascript
   document.title = '[Human] ' + document.title;
   ```
4. **Scroll to top** to ensure the page is scrolled to the very top before injection
5. **Inject** via `javascript_tool` (replace PORT with the port from step 1):
   ```javascript
   const s = document.createElement('script'); s.src = 'http://localhost:PORT/detect.js'; document.head.appendChild(s);
   ```
6. Wait 2-3 seconds for the detector to render overlays
7. **Read results from console** using `read_console_messages` with pattern `impeccable`. The detector logs all findings with the `[impeccable]` prefix. Do NOT scroll through the page to take screenshots of the overlays.
8. **Cleanup**: Stop the live server when done:
   ```bash
   npx impeccable live stop
   ```

For multi-view targets, inject on 3-5 representative pages. If injection fails, continue with CLI results only.

Return: CLI findings (JSON), browser console findings (if applicable), and any false positives noted.

### Generate Combined Critique Report

Synthesize both assessments into a single report. Do NOT simply concatenate. Weave the findings together, noting where the LLM review and detector agree, where the detector caught issues the LLM missed, and where detector findings are false positives.

Structure your feedback as a design director would:

#### Design Health Score
> *Consult [heuristics-scoring](heuristics-scoring.md)*

Present the Nielsen's 10 heuristics scores as a table:

| # | Heuristic | Score | Key Issue |
|---|-----------|-------|-----------|
| 1 | Visibility of System Status | ? | [specific finding or "n/a" if solid] |
| 2 | Match System / Real World | ? | |
| 3 | User Control and Freedom | ? | |
| 4 | Consistency and Standards | ? | |
| 5 | Error Prevention | ? | |
| 6 | Recognition Rather Than Recall | ? | |
| 7 | Flexibility and Efficiency | ? | |
| 8 | Aesthetic and Minimalist Design | ? | |
| 9 | Error Recovery | ? | |
| 10 | Help and Documentation | ? | |
| **Total** | | **??/40** | **[Rating band]** |

Be honest with scores. A 4 means genuinely excellent. Most real interfaces score 20-32.

#### Anti-Patterns Verdict

**Start here.** Does this look AI-generated?

**LLM assessment**: Your own evaluation of AI slop tells. Cover overall aesthetic feel, layout sameness, generic composition, missed opportunities for personality.

**Deterministic scan**: Summarize what the automated detector found, with counts and file locations. Note any additional issues the detector caught that you missed, and flag any false positives.

**Visual overlays** (if browser was used): Tell the user that overlays are now visible in the **[Human]** tab in their browser, highlighting the detected issues. Summarize what the console output reported.

#### Overall Impression
A brief gut reaction: what works, what doesn't, and the single biggest opportunity.

#### What's Working
Highlight 2-3 things done well. Be specific about why they work.

#### Priority Issues
The 3-5 most impactful design problems, ordered by importance.

For each issue, tag with **P0-P3 severity** (consult [heuristics-scoring](heuristics-scoring.md) for severity definitions):
- **[P?] What**: Name the problem clearly
- **Why it matters**: How this hurts users or undermines goals
- **Fix**: What to do about it (be concrete)
- **Suggested command**: Which command could address this (from: $impeccable adapt, $impeccable animate, $impeccable audit, $impeccable bolder, $impeccable clarify, $impeccable colorize, $impeccable critique, $impeccable delight, $impeccable distill, $impeccable document, $impeccable harden, $impeccable layout, $impeccable onboard, $impeccable optimize, $impeccable overdrive, $impeccable polish, $impeccable quieter, $impeccable shape, $impeccable typeset)

#### Persona Red Flags
> *Consult [personas](personas.md)*

Auto-select 2-3 personas most relevant to this interface type (use the selection table in the reference). If `AGENTS.md` contains a `## Design Context` section from `impeccable teach`, also generate 1-2 project-specific personas from the audience/brand info.

For each selected persona, walk through the primary user action and list specific red flags found:

**Alex (Power User)**: No keyboard shortcuts detected. Form requires 8 clicks for primary action. Forced modal onboarding. High abandonment risk.

**Jordan (First-Timer)**: Icon-only nav in sidebar. Technical jargon in error messages ("404 Not Found"). No visible help. Will abandon at step 2.

Be specific. Name the exact elements and interactions that fail each persona. Don't write generic persona descriptions; write what broke for them.

#### Minor Observations
Quick notes on smaller issues worth addressing.

#### Questions to Consider
Provocative questions that might unlock better solutions:
- "What if the primary action were more prominent?"
- "Does this need to feel this complex?"
- "What would a confident version of this look like?"

**Remember**:
- Be direct. Vague feedback wastes everyone's time.
- Be specific. "The submit button," not "some elements."
- Say what's wrong AND why it matters to users.
- Give concrete suggestions, not just "consider exploring..."
- Prioritize ruthlessly. If everything is important, nothing is.
- Don't soften criticism. Developers need honest feedback to ship great design.

### Ask the User

**After presenting findings**, use targeted questions based on what was actually found. STOP and use Codex's structured user-input/question tool when available; if unavailable, ask directly in chat to clarify what you cannot infer. These answers will shape the action plan.

Ask questions along these lines (adapt to the specific findings; do NOT ask generic questions):

1. **Priority direction**: Based on the issues found, ask which category matters most to the user right now. For example: "I found problems with visual hierarchy, color usage, and information overload. Which area should we tackle first?" Offer the top 2-3 issue categories as options.

2. **Design intent**: If the critique found a tonal mismatch, ask whether it was intentional. For example: "The interface feels clinical and corporate. Is that the intended tone, or should it feel warmer/bolder/more playful?" Offer 2-3 tonal directions as options based on what would fix the issues found.

3. **Scope**: Ask how much the user wants to take on. For example: "I found N issues. Want to address everything, or focus on the top 3?" Offer scope options like "Top 3 only", "All issues", "Critical issues only".

4. **Constraints** (optional; only ask if relevant): If the findings touch many areas, ask if anything is off-limits. For example: "Should any sections stay as-is?" This prevents the plan from touching things the user considers done.

**Rules for questions**:
- Every question must reference specific findings from the report. Never ask generic "who is your audience?" questions.
- Keep it to 2-4 questions maximum. Respect the user's time.
- Offer concrete options, not open-ended prompts.
- If findings are straightforward (e.g., only 1-2 clear issues), skip questions and go directly to Recommended Actions.

### Recommended Actions

**After receiving the user's answers**, present a prioritized action summary reflecting the user's priorities and scope from Ask the User.

#### Action Summary

List recommended commands in priority order, based on the user's answers:

1. **`$command-name`**: Brief description of what to fix (specific context from critique findings)
2. **`$command-name`**: Brief description (specific context)
...

**Rules for recommendations**:
- Only recommend commands from: $impeccable adapt, $impeccable animate, $impeccable audit, $impeccable bolder, $impeccable clarify, $impeccable colorize, $impeccable critique, $impeccable delight, $impeccable distill, $impeccable document, $impeccable harden, $impeccable layout, $impeccable onboard, $impeccable optimize, $impeccable overdrive, $impeccable polish, $impeccable quieter, $impeccable shape, $impeccable typeset
- Order by the user's stated priorities first, then by impact
- Each item's description should carry enough context that the command knows what to focus on
- Map each Priority Issue to the appropriate command
- Skip commands that would address zero issues
- If the user chose a limited scope, only include items within that scope
- If the user marked areas as off-limits, exclude commands that would touch those areas
- End with `$impeccable polish` as the final step if any fixes were recommended

After presenting the summary, tell the user:

> You can ask me to run these one at a time, all at once, or in any order you prefer.
>
> Re-run `$impeccable critique` after fixes to see your score improve.
